For the three terms, a pair's cosine similarity is written as a sum over KCs (the product of the KC's two trial means, divided by the norms of the two odors' response vectors), and each KC's product goes to the term for two responders, for a responder and a sub-threshold entry, or for two sub-threshold entries. The terms add up to the cosine, and a distance is one minus a cosine, so the terms' changes between the control flies and the flies with APL output blocked (TNT) add up to the pair's APL effect. The intervals come from drawing the recordings of each genotype again with replacement. A term's share of the pattern is its covariance with the whole effect across the 120 pairs divided by the variance of the whole effect, so the three shares add up to 1. The responder × sub-threshold term divides further by its sub-threshold entry: of its {{E68/data_effect_terms.csv:one__effect_mean:observed ~ +.3f}}, {{E68/data_effect_terms.csv:one_weak__effect_mean:observed ~ +.3f}} comes from weakly positive entries, {{E68/data_effect_terms.csv:one_negative__effect_mean:observed ~ +.3f}} from negative ones and {{E68/data_effect_terms.csv:one_unreliable__effect_mean:observed ~ +.3f}} from entries with a trial above 1.5 z, responses that did not repeat. Part of the sub-threshold structure is a baseline of the KC that also shows in its response to the solvent, part is weak broad tuning, and part of the noise is shared by the trials of one repeat; the negative values in particular depend on how the trial means were z-scored, which is still to be confirmed.

**The two added readings of the recordings.** Beside the four used before (as measured, sub-threshold values set to 0, binarized, corrected for trial-to-trial noise), the recordings were read with their negative values set to 0 and with every value at or below {{data:kc_noise_floor ~ .2f}} z set to 0. That limit is twice the standard deviation of the noise of a three-trial mean, from the trial-to-trial standard deviation of the control flies' non-responding entries ({{data:ctrl_trial_noise_sd ~ .2f}} z per trial; {{data:tnt_trial_noise_sd ~ .2f}} with APL output blocked (TNT)). Each reading has its own floor of the loss, computed as for the recordings as measured, and a model is always read in the same way as the recordings it is scored against.

**What was added to the model's readout.** The readout's values are put in the recordings' units by a factor c, set so that the share of the model's APL-on entries above 1 z is the control flies' response rate. The measurement noise is independent Gaussian noise of the control flies' per-trial size on every KC × odor entry, in ten recordings of three trials per model, processed as the recordings are (for the reading as measured, its expected effect on the distances is computed in closed form). The one-spike floor is the offset b in (n + b) / (n + b + k) for a KC with at least one spike; 0 stays 0. k, b and c are found together, by iteration to a fixed point, so that through the noise the model's APL-on entries have the control flies' response rate above 1 z, share between 0.5 and 1.5 z and participation ratio among responders. Nothing from the flies with APL output blocked (TNT), no distance and nothing about the APL effect enters, and the same values are applied to the APL-off counts. The offset was first to be set on the share between 0.5 and 1 z; that share hardly moves with b once c holds the response rate, the calibration ran to its bounds in most models, and the band was widened. With the wider band it still reaches a bound in part of the saved models (k in {{E68/medians.csv:config=floor_noise;reading=graded:n_K_at_bound ~ .0f}} and b in {{E68/medians.csv:config=floor_noise;reading=graded:n_floor_at_bound ~ .0f}} of the {{E68/medians.csv:config=floor_noise;reading=graded:n ~ .0f}} behind the medians), the reference model and the first joint fit not among them. Models in which fewer entries hold any spike than the control flies have responders cannot reach the recordings' response rate under any c; these {{E68/medians.csv:config=floor_noise;reading=graded:n_rate_unreachable ~ .0f}}, mostly early models tuned to 5 %, are left out of the medians. A first probe had found the first joint fit's pattern part sensitive to the weight of a single spike; that was with k held fixed, and it disappears once k is calibrated together with the offset. The model's noise is independent for every KC, odor and trial, while part of the recordings' is shared within a repeat and across a KC's odors.

**The membrane-potential terms.** Six models (the reference model, the 70 Hz fit of its family, the first joint fit and the fit of its family with breadth exponent 3, and the best fit so far under each definition from [the APL-effect fits](#apl-effect-fits), which were still running) were simulated again at their fitted parameters, with APL on and with APL removed, with every KC's membrane potential saved; their spike counts equal the saved ones entry for entry. Each KC × odor trace is reduced to its mean over the odor window, relative to the last 100 ms before the odor and as a fraction of the distance to the KC's threshold (its peak in place of the mean gives nearly the same results). For an entry without a spike the readout adds a weight times the depolarization, at most the reading of one spike; for every non-responding entry (fewer than two spikes) it subtracts a weight times the hyperpolarization. The two weights are set together with k, b and c, on the APL-on run through the noise, so that the model has the control flies' share of entries between 0.25 and 1 z ({{data:ctrl_share_z_025_1 ~ .1%}}) and below −0.25 z ({{data:ctrl_share_z_below_m025 ~ .1%}}). Three controls separate what the potential holds from what any added term does: each term alone, the potential permuted across the non-spiking KCs within each odor and condition, and the APL-on potential used for both conditions. The terms are a test of where the recordings' low end could come from in the model, not a proposed readout.

### How the pattern part of the APL-effect loss is split by odor and by pair {#fn-apl-effect-parts}

**What is done.** A model's pattern error is its per-pair APL effect minus the flies', each with its own mean over the 120 pairs removed first. An additive description over odors gives every odor one term and writes a pair's value as the sum of its two odors' terms, so that an odor contributes the same amount to each of the 15 pairs it is in. Fitted to the pattern error by least squares (16 terms for 120 pairs), it gives the per-odor error, and what it leaves over is the pair-specific error; the per-odor part and the pair-specific part are their root mean squares over the pairs. The fit is a projection, so the two errors are uncorrelated and their squares add: the squared loss is the squared mean part plus the squared per-odor part plus the squared pair-specific part. The same additive fit applied to an effect itself, the flies' or a model's, gives each odor's share of it, and the fraction of the effect's variance across pairs that the shares explain says how far the effect is a matter of single odors.

**The floors.** They are built as the floor of the loss is: the recordings of each genotype are drawn again with replacement, the resampled effect is scored against the measured one as if it were a model's, and the mean of each part over the draws is its floor, with the middle 90 % of the draws as its interval. Sixteen terms fitted to 120 noisy values take some of the noise with them, and the per-odor floor includes that.

**Why this split.** The two parts point at different kinds of mechanism. An APL that acts too strongly or too weakly on one odor moves all of that odor's pairs together and shows in the per-odor part; an error in which KCs two particular odors have in common shows in the pair-specific part. The assignment is to parts of the error, not to causes: a per-odor part can come from which KCs an odor recruits as well as from how many APL removes, which is what the oracle was built to separate.

**The per-odor APL oracle.** The model has no interaction between odors, so an odor simulated on its own reproduces its column of the 16-odor simulation exactly (checked on the reference model), and each odor can be given an APL of its own. At the fitted parameters, each odor's ORN input was first scaled until its response fraction with APL off equals that of the flies with APL output blocked (TNT); its APL→KC and APL→bouton scales were then multiplied by one factor, found by bisection on a log scale between 1/16 and 16, until its response fraction with APL on equals the control flies'. It is an [oracle](#fn-oracle): the factors and the input scales are read off the KC data the model is then scored against, and nothing in the circuit is proposed that would set them. Under the calibrated readout the half-saturation k stays the fitted model's, so the readout does not move with the oracle, nor with the breadth exponent in the variants that change it.

### How the firing ceiling works, and how its settings were chosen {#fn-firing-ceiling}

**What is done.** A model KC integrates its input and spikes when its potential crosses its threshold. Before the firing ceiling for the KCs the potential was then reset and integration resumed at once, so that nothing but the input limited how often a KC could fire. Two things were added to the model's KCs, both off unless set, so that every other model on this page is as it was. A refractory period: for 3 ms after a spike the potential is held at its reset value and no spike is possible. And spike-triggered adaptation: each KC carries a variable that is raised by a fixed increment at each of its spikes and decays back to zero with a time constant, and the KC spikes when its potential exceeds its threshold multiplied by one plus that variable. The increment is thus a fraction of the KC's own threshold and keeps its meaning when the thresholds are re-tuned. In a run without feedback the ceiling can only remove spikes, and leaves every KC × odor entry of fewer than two spikes as it was; in the full model the response rates shift a little through APL and the KC–KC inhibition, and at every setting both were brought back to the base model's own by two re-tunings in turn: the somatic threshold on the response rate with APL off, then one common factor on the APL→KC and APL→bouton scales on the response rate with APL on, the KC–KC scale left as fitted.

**The time constant and the strength.** With a time constant of 1 s, twice the length of the odor, the adaptation hardly decays within a response: every spike makes the next one harder, and the ceiling is a budget of spikes per odor more than a limit on the firing rate. The time constant matters more than the strength. On the current reference model three strengths were run at time constants of 50 ms, 200 ms and 1 s, each at fixed scales with both response rates held. At 50 ms the adaptation recovers within the odor and the APL-effect loss on raw counts stays between {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_r3_a3_t50:apl_raw_rmse ~ .3f}} and {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_r3_a1_t50:apl_raw_rmse ~ .3f}}, from {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_base:apl_raw_rmse ~ .3f}} without a ceiling (lower is better; floor {{data:apl_effect_rmse_floor ~ .3f}}); at 200 ms it is between {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_r3_a3_t200:apl_raw_rmse ~ .3f}} and {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_r3_a1_t200:apl_raw_rmse ~ .3f}}, and at 1 s between {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_r3_a2_t1000:apl_raw_rmse ~ .3f}} and {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_r3_a1_t1000:apl_raw_rmse ~ .3f}}. The refractory period alone leaves it at {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_r3:apl_raw_rmse ~ .3f}}. The three strengths were set in a pilot on the reference model with APL off, at the 1 s time constant, so that its largest spike count comes to about 40, 20 and 10: increments of {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_r3_a1_t1000:kc_adapt_inc ~ .3f}}, {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_r3_a2_t1000:kc_adapt_inc ~ .3f}} and {{E69/stats_variants.csv:ref40_r3_a3_t1000:kc_adapt_inc ~ .3f}} of the threshold per spike.

**The two settings that were fitted.** The literature gives no largest spike count for a KC, only means and standard deviations per response: {{lit:4.9 ± 3.0 # spikes per response of α′/β′ KCs to a 500 ms odor (mean ± standard deviation, whole-cell recordings, APL intact), Turner, Bazhenov and Laurent (2008), as quoted in E69_kc_firing_ceiling.md, Design (part B), Realism and Caveats}} spikes for α′/β′ KCs and {{lit:2.2 ± 1.2 # spikes per response of α/β KCs to a 500 ms odor (mean ± standard deviation, whole-cell recordings, APL intact), Turner, Bazhenov and Laurent (2008), as quoted in E69_kc_firing_ceiling.md, Realism and Caveats}} for α/β KCs in whole-cell recordings with a 0.5 s odor (Turner, Bazhenov and Laurent, 2008; other odors and concentrations than the KC data's, APL intact). The bound used is the mean plus three standard deviations of the α′/β′ KCs, about {{lit:14 # the bound on a KC's spike count to one odor with APL on that the firing ceiling for the KCs takes from whole-cell recordings: the mean plus three standard deviations (4.9 + 3 x 3.0 = 13.9) of the spikes per response of α′/β′ KCs to a 500 ms odor in Turner, Bazhenov and Laurent (2008); E69_kc_firing_ceiling.md, Design (part B) and Caveats}} spikes, a reading that a measured largest count would replace. The literature setting is the weakest of the three strengths, at which the largest count of the breadth-2 APL-effect fit with APL on ({{E69/stats_variants.csv:e67cal_r3_a1_t1000:max_count_on ~ .0f}} at fixed scales, {{E69/stats_variants.csv:rate0.068_aplboth_refr3_adapt0.04142t1000_k2:max_count_on ~ .0f}} once fitted) is nearest that bound. The stronger setting is the middle strength, the weakest at which the half-saturation of [the calibrated readout](#fn-calibrated-readout) runs to the upper end of its range for that fit: the model's counts with APL on are then no more concentrated than the control flies' responses, the readout has nothing to compress, and the two definitions of the model's side give the same scores. There the largest count of the breadth-2 fit with APL on is {{E69/stats_variants.csv:rate0.068_aplboth_refr3_adapt0.1114t1000_k2:max_count_on ~ .0f}} spikes and the 99th percentile of its responders' counts {{E69/stats_variants.csv:rate0.068_aplboth_refr3_adapt0.1114t1000_k2:p99_resp_count_on ~ .0f}}, about the mean of the α′/β′ KCs in the recordings, so the setting allows those KCs less than they show; in the reference model, whose counts are higher, the same strength gives a largest count of {{E69/stats_variants.csv:thr40_gain1.5_rate0.068_aplboth_refr3_adapt0.1114t1000_k2:max_count_on ~ .0f}}. Each fit is the joint fit under the APL-effect loss with the term on raw counts and the term under the calibrated readout at equal weight, the response rates held as constraints at the flies' response rate, the ceiling fixed and the usual four parameters free (the somatic threshold, the APL→KC scale with the KC→APL scale tied to it, the APL→bouton scale up to three times the baseline's, and the KC–KC scale), started from the run of its setting at fixed scales. For the breadth-2 family the fit without a ceiling is that family's fit with both definitions in the loss, which had ended at practically the same model as its fit under the readout, the one in the best-models table. Every fit simulated again at its fitted parameters reproduces its saved spike counts exactly.

**What the ceiling is not.** One adaptation variable per KC with one time constant is the simplest form that bounds a count. Real KC adaptation has several time scales (calcium-activated potassium currents, synaptic depression at the claws), and a time constant longer than the odor makes the model's version close to a cap on the number of spikes per odor; whether the result needs that, or holds with a less schematic adaptation, has not been tested. The strength stays a choice until the largest spike count of a fly KC to a 0.5 s odor is measured.

### What "baseline-noise units" are, and the response criterion of the realism bands {#fn-baseline-noise-units}

**What the baseline noise of a KC is.** A KC's signal in a recording is its fluorescence trace with 0.7 times the fluorescence of the neuropil around it subtracted, neither detrended nor divided by a resting fluorescence. A recording holds 51 odor presentations (the 16 odors and the solvent, three times each), and in the 5 s before each of them no odor is present. The baseline noise of a KC is the standard deviation of its signal within those 51 pre-odor windows, taken after each window's own mean is subtracted from its values and pooled over the windows (the summed squares divided by the number of values less the 51 means removed). Subtracting each window's mean keeps the slow drift of the trace over the {{lit:40 # about 7,194 imaging volumes at 2.96 volumes per second, 40.5 minutes per recording: notes/yang_response_normalization/recordings.csv, columns n_volumes and volume_rate}} minutes of a recording out of it, and the windows hold no odor response, so what is left is how much the KC's signal fluctuates from one imaging volume to another when nothing is presented: its measurement noise. It is one number per KC, computed from the raw traces for every KC of the {{data:n_recordings_ctrl ~ .0f}} recordings of control flies and the {{data:n_recordings_tnt ~ .0f}} recordings with APL output blocked (TNT).

**A response in baseline-noise units.** A trial's response is the mean of the KC's signal from 2 to 8 s after odor onset minus its mean over the 5 s before, and the response to an odor is the mean over the odor's three trials, the same windows and the same mean as in the recordings' own analysis. Divided by the KC's baseline noise, it is the response in baseline-noise units: by how many times its own noise the KC's signal rose. The recordings' own values, the ones used everywhere else on this page, are the same differences divided by something else, the standard deviation of the KC's signal over the whole recording (a z-score of the whole trace). A response in baseline-noise units is therefore also the z-scored response multiplied by the ratio of the KC's whole-trace standard deviation to its baseline noise. Recomputed from the raw traces, the z-scored traces and the per-trial responses agree with those saved with the recordings, and with the values this page uses, to the rounding of the stored numbers in every recording.

**Why the recordings' own unit is not a fixed one.** One z is the standard deviation of a KC's signal over its whole recording, which holds the KC's noise, its slow drift and its own odor responses. The unit is therefore larger in a KC that responds to more odors, or more strongly: the median ratio of the whole-trace standard deviation to the baseline noise is {{data:kc_scale_ratio_0 ~ .1f}} for the KCs that respond to none of the 16 odors, {{data:kc_scale_ratio_1 ~ .1f}} for those that respond to one, {{data:kc_scale_ratio_3 ~ .1f}} to three, {{data:kc_scale_ratio_6 ~ .1f}} to six, {{data:kc_scale_ratio_8 ~ .1f}} to eight and {{data:kc_scale_ratio_11plus ~ .1f}} to eleven or more (both genotypes together, the number of odors counted by the recordings' criterion). The recordings' criterion for a response, a three-trial mean above 1 z, is thus relative to each KC: the same change in fluorescence over the same noise counts as a response in a KC that responds to little else and not in one that responds to many odors. A model KC's criterion, two or more spikes to the odor, does not depend on what else the KC does.

**The threshold.** In baseline-noise units one threshold serves every KC of every recording: a response is a three-trial mean above {{data:noise_threshold ~ .2f}} times the KC's baseline noise. That value is the one at which the control flies' response rate (the share of KC × odor entries that respond, averaged over the recordings) equals their response rate by the recordings' own criterion, {{data:ctrl_frac ~ .3f}}; it was found on the control recordings alone and is applied unchanged to the recordings with APL output blocked (TNT). The two criteria coincide for a KC whose whole-trace standard deviation is that many times its baseline noise, about the median of the KCs that respond to three or four odors ({{data:kc_scale_ratio_3 ~ .2f}} and {{data:kc_scale_ratio_4 ~ .2f}}); for a KC below that ratio the criterion in baseline-noise units is the stricter of the two, for a KC above it the more lenient. The threshold is a calibration and not a significance level: the baseline noise is the fluctuation of single imaging volumes, whereas a response is a difference between two window means averaged over three trials.

**How the realism bands use it.** Since 2026-11-10 all four bands count a response in a model as two or more spikes to the odor, as before, and a response in the flies as a three-trial mean above the threshold in baseline-noise units. Each side then has a criterion that is the same for all of its KCs, and the two are tied at one point: the threshold holds the control flies at their measured response rate, and the models are tuned to their response rate with APL on by the two-spike count. The band on the response rate with APL on thus tests a model's own firing, and the other three compare what follows from it on each side: how many more responses there are without APL (the ratio of the response rates), which odors have them (response rate ρ, against the control flies' per-odor response rates counted in baseline-noise units) and how they are spread over the KCs (the distances between the distributions of odors per KC, with APL on against the control flies and with APL off against the flies with APL output blocked (TNT), the band being the most atypical recording's distance from the other recordings of its genotype, all counted in baseline-noise units). The limits of the three bands on the response rates are as they were. No model was simulated again: the change is on the flies' side, and each saved model was scored again from its saved responses.

**Why not the recordings' own criterion on the flies' side.** Until 2026-11-10 the bands compared the models' two-spike responses with the flies' responses above 1 z: a fixed criterion against one that grows with how much a KC responds. The difference between the two criteria is of the size the band on odors per KC is meant to detect. Counted by the two criteria, the same flies' distributions of odors per KC are {{data:noise_ctrl_odors_per_kc_tv_vs_z ~ .3f}} apart in control flies and {{data:noise_tnt_odors_per_kc_tv_vs_z ~ .3f}} with APL output blocked (TNT) (the distance of the main text, half the summed absolute differences between the five shares), against bands of {{data:ctrl_odors_per_kc_tv_max ~ .3f}} and {{data:tnt_odors_per_kc_tv_max ~ .3f}} by that criterion: a model with exactly the flies' distribution in fixed units would have used up about two fifths of the band before any error of its own. The z-score makes the criterion more lenient for KCs that respond to little and stricter for KCs that respond to much, so by it fewer KCs are silent and fewer respond to eight or more odors (table below), and a model was held to a distribution with both ends drawn in. The current reference model was outside the band with APL off by that criterion and is inside it by the present one.

**Why not put the models through the recordings' normalization instead.** The other way of comparing like with like is to read each model as the recordings were made: each model KC's spike counts are placed in a simulated recording with the recordings' trial structure, noise and drift, the trace is z-scored over the whole session, and a response is a value above 1 z. That keeps the flies' values as measured, but the simulated noise has a level that must be set, and it is set, for each model, so that the model's response rate with APL on is the control flies'. The band on that response rate can then not fail, whatever the model's firing. A fit whose response rates were counted through this reading weakened its APL and KC-KC inhibition, its somatic threshold hardly moving, until {{apl_effect/by_variant.csv:folder=E74_apl_effect_by_odors_per_kc;tag=zcaplrate_rate0.068_aplzcell_refr3_adapt0.04142t1000_thrin0.25_k2:rate_on ~ .3f}} of its KC × odor entries held two or more spikes with APL on, against the flies' {{data:ctrl_frac ~ .3f}} and the band's upper limit of 0.085, and was inside all four bands as read. The reading also enlarges a broadly responding model KC's unit less than the recordings enlarge a fly KC's, so part of the mismatch stays, and its verdicts shift with its settings. It is kept as a second description of a model, reported beside the bands where a model has been read that way; it does not decide them, and fits hold their response rates on the two-spike count.

The KC data under the two response criteria (means over the recordings of a genotype; a cell with two values gives control flies / APL output blocked (TNT))

| statistic | the recordings' z-scored units<br>response above 1 z | baseline-noise units<br>response above {{data:noise_threshold ~ .2f}} times the baseline noise |
|---|---|---|
| response rate, control flies / APL output blocked (TNT) | {{data:ctrl_frac ~ .3f}} / {{data:tnt_frac ~ .3f}} | {{data:noise_ctrl_frac ~ .3f}} / {{data:noise_tnt_frac ~ .3f}} |
| response rate with APL output blocked (TNT) relative to control flies | {{data:rate_ratio_fraction ~ .2f}} | {{data:noise_rate_ratio_fraction ~ .2f}} |
| 90 % interval of that ratio over recordings | {{data:rate_ratio_fraction.lo ~ .1f}} to {{data:rate_ratio_fraction.hi ~ .1f}} | {{data:noise_rate_ratio_fraction.lo ~ .1f}} to {{data:noise_rate_ratio_fraction.hi ~ .1f}} |
| KCs responding to none of the 16 odors, control flies / APL output blocked (TNT) | {{data:ctrl_odors_per_kc_0 ~ .1%}} / {{data:tnt_odors_per_kc_0 ~ .1%}} | {{data:noise_ctrl_odors_per_kc_0 ~ .1%}} / {{data:noise_tnt_odors_per_kc_0 ~ .1%}} |
| to one odor | {{data:ctrl_odors_per_kc_1 ~ .1%}} / {{data:tnt_odors_per_kc_1 ~ .1%}} | {{data:noise_ctrl_odors_per_kc_1 ~ .1%}} / {{data:noise_tnt_odors_per_kc_1 ~ .1%}} |
| to two or three | {{data:ctrl_odors_per_kc_2_3 ~ .1%}} / {{data:tnt_odors_per_kc_2_3 ~ .1%}} | {{data:noise_ctrl_odors_per_kc_2_3 ~ .1%}} / {{data:noise_tnt_odors_per_kc_2_3 ~ .1%}} |
| to four to seven | {{data:ctrl_odors_per_kc_4_7 ~ .1%}} / {{data:tnt_odors_per_kc_4_7 ~ .1%}} | {{data:noise_ctrl_odors_per_kc_4_7 ~ .1%}} / {{data:noise_tnt_odors_per_kc_4_7 ~ .1%}} |
| to eight or more | {{data:ctrl_odors_per_kc_8plus ~ .1%}} / {{data:tnt_odors_per_kc_8plus ~ .1%}} | {{data:noise_ctrl_odors_per_kc_8plus ~ .1%}} / {{data:noise_tnt_odors_per_kc_8plus ~ .1%}} |
| odors per KC: distance of one recording from the mean of the others, median over recordings | {{data:ctrl_odors_per_kc_tv_median ~ .3f}} / {{data:tnt_odors_per_kc_tv_median ~ .3f}} | {{data:noise_ctrl_odors_per_kc_tv_median ~ .3f}} / {{data:noise_tnt_odors_per_kc_tv_median ~ .3f}} |
| odors per KC: the same distance, largest (the realism band: the right-hand column since 2026-11-10, the left-hand one before) | {{data:ctrl_odors_per_kc_tv_max ~ .3f}} / {{data:tnt_odors_per_kc_tv_max ~ .3f}} | {{data:noise_ctrl_odors_per_kc_tv_max ~ .3f}} / {{data:noise_tnt_odors_per_kc_tv_max ~ .3f}} |

**What changes in the flies' values.** The overall response rates hardly depend on the unit. The control flies' is the same by construction; with APL output blocked (TNT) it is a little higher in baseline-noise units, and the ratio with it, well inside the unchanged band on the ratio. The per-odor response rates keep their order: across the 16 odors the rates by the two criteria correlate at ρ={{data:noise_ctrl_frac_rho_vs_z ~ .2f}} in control flies and {{data:noise_tnt_frac_rho_vs_z ~ .2f}} with APL output blocked (TNT), so a model's response rate ρ changes little. What changes is how the responses are spread over the KCs: in baseline-noise units more KCs respond to nothing and more respond to eight or more odors, in both genotypes, and fewer respond to between one and three. The band on odors per KC is a little narrower with APL on and wider with APL off. Outside the realism bands, the response rates of the flies given on this page (overall, per odor, and in every response rate ρ of a table) remain by the recordings' own criterion, by which the experiments were run and scored.

**Caveats.** The baseline noise has been computed only for the recordings of this odor panel, those behind the two KC matrices; the other KC recordings this page refers to (the low-concentration panels, the wild-type recordings) are still in their own units, and no band uses them. A baseline-noise unit is not a unit of activity: the signal is not divided by a resting fluorescence, so a response in these units still depends on how bright a KC is, and the KCs that respond to many odors are the brighter ones on average; the baseline noise itself is no larger in those KCs than in the ones that respond to nothing, and does not differ between the genotypes by more than the recordings differ among themselves. Spikes and noise-normalized fluorescence are both fixed units but not the same unit: what ties them is the calibration at the control flies' response rate, at that one point, and the flies' responses are counted through measurement noise where the models' are not. The main target is not touched by any of this: the flies' odor-odor distances, and with them the per-pair APL effect and its floor, are computed from the recordings' z-scored values as before, and whether they too should be taken in baseline-noise units is a separate question.
